English for Work · Health & life sciences

Pharmacy Technician English

Clarify prescription details, explain claim and refill statuses, coordinate stock queries, protect privacy, and hand medication questions to pharmacists with precise, practical English.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book

For Pharmacy technicians and pharmacy support staff.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Identify an ambiguous order field and arrange pharmacist clarification without selecting a strength from memory or appearance.
  • Explain an insurance response accurately and connect the patient with an accepted status query without promising coverage.
  • Explain an unresolved renewal and make a reliable communication commitment without promising prescriber response or medicine availability.
  • Explain a stock shortage, qualify a supplier forecast, and route alternative-treatment questions without choosing a substitute.
  • Recognize an auxiliary-label question and complete a clear pharmacist handoff without interpreting or changing the instruction.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All cases, calculations, and product limits are fictional language exercises, not patient-specific directions or clinical certification. Follow local law, authorized duties, pharmacist direction, privacy procedures, and actual recall notices. Urgent concerns need the appropriate prompt response, not a routine update window. US and UK sources provide context, not interchangeable rules. This book does not teach prescribing, dose selection, or clinical counseling.

01Prescription intake without assumptionsA familiar label does not resolve an unclear strength+

Your goal

Identify an ambiguous order field and arrange pharmacist clarification without selecting a strength from memory or appearance.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

A familiar label does not resolve an unclear strength

Patient Ellis asks technician Nia to complete a prescription order whose strength field is unclear. Ellis remembers a blue label and says the prescriber usually orders the same thing. Nia cannot select the strength. Pharmacist Maya can take the clarification, but no completion time is known. Nia must distinguish remembered information from verified current instructions, explain the unresolved field, and route the question. No medicine name, dose, or numerical strength is provided in this teaching case, and the exercise does not ask the learner to infer one.

Ellis: Patient / Nia: Pharmacy technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which field is unclear?

Answer and explanation

B. Strength

The current order has an unclear strength field, which requires appropriate clarification.

2. What does Ellis remember?

Answer and explanation

C. A blue label and a usual prescription pattern

The remembered label and pattern are reported context, not verified current instructions.

3. Who can take the clarification?

Answer and explanation

B. Pharmacist Maya

Maya is the identified pharmacist route, while Nia cannot choose the strength.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Strength and dose are not synonyms. A product may contain a particular amount per unit, while the prescribed administration uses a specified amount and schedule. This unit supplies neither and does not authorize calculation or selection from a remembered label.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
prescription
An authorized order for a medicine under the applicable rules.verify a prescription
intake
Receiving and organizing information needed to process an order.complete prescription intake
prescriber
A professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine.contact the prescriber
pharmacist
A qualified medicines professional with the relevant practice authority.refer to the pharmacist
strength
The amount of active ingredient in a stated unit or quantity of the product.clarify the strength
dose
The amount prescribed for a particular administration.distinguish dose from strength
dosage form
The physical form of the product, such as a tablet or liquid.verify the dosage form
active ingredient
The substance intended to produce the medicine's therapeutic effect.identify the active ingredient
concentration
The amount of a substance within a specified quantity of a preparation.verify the concentration
route
The specified way a medicine is taken or applied.verify the route
frequency
How often a prescribed action or administration occurs.clarify the frequency
directions for use
The authorized instructions for taking or using the medicine.verify directions for use
quantity
The amount of product specified for supply.confirm the quantity
days supply
Days represented by quantity and verified directions under the relevant calculation rules; not proof of actual use.verify days supply
brand name
A trade name for a particular product.confirm the brand name
generic name
The recognized nonproprietary name of the medicine substance.identify the generic name
product identifier
A code or reference identifying a specified product.verify the product identifier
NDC
National Drug Code; a United States drug product identification system.check the NDC
order field
A defined part of the order record.clarify an order field
ambiguous entry
Information that permits more than one interpretation.flag an ambiguous entry
clarification request
A request to resolve an unclear or missing instruction.route a clarification request
current order
The order presently being processed, distinct from past records.verify the current order
historical record
Information about a previous order or event.consult the historical record
verified instruction
A direction confirmed through the appropriate process.preserve the verified instruction

Previous records may provide relevant context for the pharmacist, but they do not automatically resolve an unclear current order. Preserve what is uncertain and who supplied each detail. A familiar color, usual routine, or urgent request is not a verified instruction.

C Notice the language

Accuracy can sound helpful rather than obstructive

A person who takes a medicine regularly may expect recognition to be enough. Acknowledge that familiarity and explain the exact unresolved detail. Give a pharmacist route instead of asking the patient to guess the prescription or treating the uncertainty as their fault.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the unresolved field
The strength field on this order is unclear.
Acknowledge familiarity
I understand that you usually receive the same medicine.
Separate memory and confirmation
The label color is useful context, but it does not confirm the current strength.
State the role
I cannot select a strength from an unclear entry.
Offer the next step
Pharmacist Maya can take the clarification.
Avoid blame
We need the order clarified; I am not asking you to guess it.
Preserve the source
I will record that you remember a blue label.
Distinguish terms
Strength describes the product; dose concerns the prescribed amount used.
Check the current order
We need the current instructions confirmed, not just an earlier pattern.
Route history appropriately
The pharmacist can review the relevant previous record.
Avoid a time promise
I do not yet have a confirmed completion time.
Keep status accurate
The clarification is pending, not completed.
Ask about the accepted handoff
Can Maya confirm receipt of this question?
Keep clinical advice separate
Any question about how to take it should go to the pharmacist.
Report an urgent concern
Please tell the pharmacist about any concern that needs prompt attention.
Close with a clear boundary
We will use the verified instruction rather than fill the gap with a guess.
6 language and precision notes
This order
Anchors the question to the current prescription rather than a general habit.
Useful context, but
Acknowledges the patient's contribution while preserving its evidential limit.
Cannot select
Names the specific prohibited action in this role.
Not asking you to guess
Avoids shifting the uncertainty onto the patient.
Do not yet have
Preserves timing uncertainty without inventing a deadline.
Verified instruction
Identifies the required result of clarification, not the technician's preferred answer.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which reply is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. The strength is unclear; Maya can clarify it, and no completion time is confirmed.

The reply identifies the unresolved detail, appropriate route, and genuine timing uncertainty.

2. Which distinction is correct?

Answer and explanation

A. Strength describes product content per unit or quantity; dose describes the prescribed amount used.

These terms describe different information and cannot be substituted without creating ambiguity.

3. What should happen to the patient's recollection?

Answer and explanation

A. Record it as reported context for the pharmacist, not verified current instructions.

The recollection may help clarification while remaining distinct from an authorized current instruction.

4. What must Nia avoid promising?

Answer and explanation

D. A completion time that has not been confirmed

The brief provides no known completion time, so a definite timing promise would be unsupported.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

A familiar label does not resolve an unclear strength

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ellis: Patient / Nia: Pharmacy technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • current order
  • directions for use
  • clarification request
  • dose
  • ambiguous entry
  • strength
  • verified instruction
  • intake
  • pharmacist
  • historical record
  1. Ellis
    Nia, this should be the same prescription I usually collect. I remember the blue label, so can you finish it while I wait?
  2. Nia
    I understand, but the 1 field on this order is unclear. I cannot select it from the color or usual pattern; pharmacist Maya can take the clarification.
  3. Ellis
    I cannot remember the number. I could recognize the box, but I do not want to guess a strength and send you in the wrong direction.
  4. Nia
    You do not need to guess the 2. I will explain the specific missing detail and pass on what you remember as context, not ask you to choose between possible entries.
  5. Ellis
    The prescriber has ordered the same thing for a long time. Would looking at the previous record settle it?
  6. Nia
    A 3 may help the pharmacist review the question, but it does not automatically replace unclear current instructions. We need the appropriate clarification rather than assume no change could have occurred.
  7. Ellis
    I sometimes mix up strength and dose. Are those two words referring to the same part of the prescription?
  8. Nia
    No. The 4 is the prescribed amount used, while strength describes the product content per unit or quantity. I cannot determine either missing instruction from the label color.
  9. Ellis
    That explains why my description was not enough. I can tell Maya what I remember, but I cannot verify what the prescriber intended this time.
  10. Nia
    I will route a 5 with that distinction intact. Your recollection can be included without being converted into a confirmed direction or a numerical value you did not provide.
  11. Ellis
    Please make sure the question goes to Maya rather than just sit in a general note. I do not want to repeat everything at another counter.
  12. Nia
    I can give the 6 the exact question and check that it is received. A handoff should identify who has the clarification, not merely say that someone will look at it.
  13. Ellis
    Can you tell me how long that will take? I need to decide whether to wait here or arrange another trip.
  14. Nia
    I do not have a confirmed completion time for this 7. I can ask about the update route, but I should not promise a collection time before the relevant information is verified.
  15. Ellis
    Thanks. I have a bus to catch, so a guessed ten minutes would leave me in a difficult position.
  16. Nia
    Agreed. A 8 is still needed. We will distinguish receiving the question, completing clarification, and completing the remaining pharmacy checks instead of treating them as one event.
  17. Ellis
    If I have a question about how to take the medicine once the order is clarified, should I ask Maya that as well?
  18. Nia
    Yes. Questions about the 9 need the pharmacist's appropriate explanation. Resolving the strength field does not mean every question about your medicine has already been answered.
  19. Ellis
    Thank you. Please tell Maya that I remember a blue label and a usual pattern, but that I have not supplied a confirmed current strength.
  20. Nia
    I will preserve that in 10. The strength remains unclear, Maya is the clarification route, and no completion time is confirmed. We will not fill the gap with an assumption.

Answers and explanations
  1. strength Turn 2. Strength identifies product content per unit or quantity, and the current unclear field cannot be resolved from label color.
  2. current order Turn 4. The current order is the prescription being processed now, which needs its actual instructions clarified.
  3. historical record Turn 6. A historical record may provide context for the pharmacist but does not automatically replace unclear current instructions.
  4. dose Turn 8. Dose concerns the prescribed amount used for an administration, while strength describes the product's content per unit or quantity.
  5. clarification request Turn 10. The clarification request identifies the unresolved field and asks the appropriate professional to establish the intended current information.
  6. pharmacist Turn 12. The pharmacist is the identified professional route for resolving the order question rather than leaving the technician to select a strength.
  7. ambiguous entry Turn 14. The ambiguous entry remains unresolved, and its clarification process has no confirmed completion time in the supplied facts.
  8. verified instruction Turn 16. A verified instruction is the appropriately confirmed direction needed to resolve the order, not an expected answer or timing promise.
  9. directions for use Turn 18. Directions for use concern the authorized use of the medicine and should be addressed through the pharmacist's appropriate clinical discussion.
  10. intake Turn 20. Intake organizes the current request and its sources while keeping the unresolved field visible for the proper clarification process.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ellis: Nia, this should be the same prescription I usually collect. I remember the blue label, so can you finish it while I wait?
  2. Nia: I understand, but the strength field on this order is unclear. I cannot select it from the color or usual pattern; pharmacist Maya can take the clarification.
  3. Ellis: I cannot remember the number. I could recognize the box, but I do not want to guess a strength and send you in the wrong direction.
  4. Nia: You do not need to guess the current order. I will explain the specific missing detail and pass on what you remember as context, not ask you to choose between possible entries.
  5. Ellis: The prescriber has ordered the same thing for a long time. Would looking at the previous record settle it?
  6. Nia: A historical record may help the pharmacist review the question, but it does not automatically replace unclear current instructions. We need the appropriate clarification rather than assume no change could have occurred.
  7. Ellis: I sometimes mix up strength and dose. Are those two words referring to the same part of the prescription?
  8. Nia: No. The dose is the prescribed amount used, while strength describes the product content per unit or quantity. I cannot determine either missing instruction from the label color.
  9. Ellis: That explains why my description was not enough. I can tell Maya what I remember, but I cannot verify what the prescriber intended this time.
  10. Nia: I will route a clarification request with that distinction intact. Your recollection can be included without being converted into a confirmed direction or a numerical value you did not provide.
  11. Ellis: Please make sure the question goes to Maya rather than just sit in a general note. I do not want to repeat everything at another counter.
  12. Nia: I can give the pharmacist the exact question and check that it is received. A handoff should identify who has the clarification, not merely say that someone will look at it.
  13. Ellis: Can you tell me how long that will take? I need to decide whether to wait here or arrange another trip.
  14. Nia: I do not have a confirmed completion time for this ambiguous entry. I can ask about the update route, but I should not promise a collection time before the relevant information is verified.
  15. Ellis: Thanks. I have a bus to catch, so a guessed ten minutes would leave me in a difficult position.
  16. Nia: Agreed. A verified instruction is still needed. We will distinguish receiving the question, completing clarification, and completing the remaining pharmacy checks instead of treating them as one event.
  17. Ellis: If I have a question about how to take the medicine once the order is clarified, should I ask Maya that as well?
  18. Nia: Yes. Questions about the directions for use need the pharmacist's appropriate explanation. Resolving the strength field does not mean every question about your medicine has already been answered.
  19. Ellis: Thank you. Please tell Maya that I remember a blue label and a usual pattern, but that I have not supplied a confirmed current strength.
  20. Nia: I will preserve that in intake. The strength remains unclear, Maya is the clarification route, and no completion time is confirmed. We will not fill the gap with an assumption.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The blue label does not settle the strength

Fictional counter exchange using the lesson case; pharmacist Maya is available.

  1. Patient: The last box had a blue label. Can't you use that to identify the strength?
  2. Technician: The current strength field is unclear. Label color doesn't confirm the detail we need.
  3. Patient: My prescriber usually orders the same thing. I've collected it here for years.
  4. Technician: That history helps explain your question, but Maya, our pharmacist, needs to handle this clarification.
  5. Patient: Does that mean you have cancelled today's prescription because I can't remember the number?
  6. Technician: No, it is awaiting clarification. Cancelled and awaiting clarification are different statuses in our record.
  7. Patient: Could Maya look at it? I don't want to lose my place by leaving.
  8. Technician: I'll give Maya the unclear order and your question. We have no collection time yet.
  9. Patient: Please tell her I can wait for an explanation, but not indefinitely today.
  10. Technician: Certainly. I'll include that in the handoff; please stay here while I speak with her.
One letter apart

Fictional intake desk; two similar names appear, and neither record has been changed.

  1. Technician: Two similar patient profiles appear. Let's verify your details privately before selecting either record.
  2. Patient: Mine has an extra letter near the end. People often miss it when they type.
  3. Technician: Thank you. Please give your full name and date of birth here at this quieter counter.
  4. Patient: It's Elena Maren, born the fourteenth of June, nineteen eighty. Is that the same record?
  5. Technician: Those details match one profile. The other result has a different birth date.
  6. Patient: I moved recently, though. The address on that profile might still be my old one.
  7. Technician: We can check that separately. Which address should the registration team review with you?
  8. Patient: The address ending in Cedar Lane is old; my current address is on Willow Road.
  9. Technician: I'll mark the address question for review without joining the two similar profiles together.
  10. Patient: Good. I was worried the spelling difference meant all my prescriptions had disappeared.
Receipt before readiness

Fictional patient shows a received notification; the live record says pharmacist review pending.

  1. Patient: My phone says you received the prescription. I thought that meant I could collect it.
  2. Technician: That message confirms receipt. Today's record still shows pharmacist review pending, rather than ready for pickup.
  3. Patient: I have twenty minutes before work. Is that enough time for the review?
  4. Technician: I don't have a completion estimate. You don't need to stay just to keep it in the queue.
  5. Patient: Will the next notification actually say ready, or will it use the same wording?
  6. Technician: A pickup notification says ready. Please check that wording before making another trip.
  7. Patient: I changed my telephone number last month. Could that message go to the wrong number?
  8. Technician: Let's verify your contact preference privately. I won't read the number across the counter.
  9. Patient: The number ending in forty-two is current. Please check that one against my profile.
  10. Technician: It matches. The review is still pending, and the pickup notice will use that number.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A familiar label does not resolve an unclear strength
  1. Read the strength question aloud without guessing a number or naming a medicine.
  2. Complete the transfer with dosage form, the reported box description, the accepting pharmacist, and the actual timing status.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify an unclear dosage form

A current order has an unclear dosage-form field. The patient remembers a white box. Pharmacist Ren accepts the clarification. No product form or completion time has been confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: "The unclear field concerns the ___."

Answer and explanation

B. dosage form

The unresolved field is the product's physical form, not a verified strength.

2. Patient: "I remember a ___."

Answer and explanation

C. white box

The white box is reported memory and does not establish the product form.

3. Technician: "The clarification was accepted by ___."

Answer and explanation

C. Ren

Ren is the named pharmacist who accepted the question.

4. Technician: "Completion timing remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unconfirmed

The brief supplies no confirmed completion time for the clarification or order.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Prescription intake without assumptions
Original fictional case: Patient Ellis asks technician Nia to complete a prescription order whose strength field is unclear. Ellis remembers a blue label and says the prescriber usually orders the same thing. Nia cannot select the strength. Pharmacist Maya can take the clarification, but no completion time is known. Nia must distinguish remembered information from verified current instructions, explain the unresolved field, and route the question. No medicine name, dose, or numerical strength is provided in this teaching case, and the exercise does not ask the learner to infer one.
Language workshop: A familiar label does not resolve an unclear strength
Communication goal: Identify an ambiguous order field and arrange pharmacist clarification without selecting a strength from memory or appearance.
Language guidance: Strength and dose are not synonyms. A product may contain a particular amount per unit, while the prescribed administration uses a specified amount and schedule. This unit supplies neither and does not authorize calculation or selection from a remembered label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The strength field on this order is unclear. / I understand that you usually receive the same medicine. / The label color is useful context, but it does not confirm the current strength. / I cannot select a strength from an unclear entry. / Pharmacist Maya can take the clarification. / We need the order clarified; I am not asking you to guess it. / I will record that you remember a blue label. / Strength describes the product; dose concerns the prescribed amount used. / We need the current instructions confirmed, not just an earlier pattern. / The pharmacist can review the relevant previous record. / I do not yet have a confirmed completion time. / The clarification is pending, not completed. / Can Maya confirm receipt of this question? / Any question about how to take it should go to the pharmacist. / Please tell the pharmacist about any concern that needs prompt attention. / We will use the verified instruction rather than fill the gap with a guess.
Vocabulary: prescription: An authorized order for a medicine under the applicable rules. / intake: Receiving and organizing information needed to process an order. / prescriber: A professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine. / pharmacist: A qualified medicines professional with the relevant practice authority. / strength: The amount of active ingredient in a stated unit or quantity of the product. / dose: The amount prescribed for a particular administration. / dosage form: The physical form of the product, such as a tablet or liquid. / active ingredient: The substance intended to produce the medicine's therapeutic effect. / concentration: The amount of a substance within a specified quantity of a preparation. / route: The specified way a medicine is taken or applied. / frequency: How often a prescribed action or administration occurs. / directions for use: The authorized instructions for taking or using the medicine. / quantity: The amount of product specified for supply. / days supply: Days represented by quantity and verified directions under the relevant calculation rules; not proof of actual use. / brand name: A trade name for a particular product. / generic name: The recognized nonproprietary name of the medicine substance. / product identifier: A code or reference identifying a specified product. / NDC: National Drug Code; a United States drug product identification system. / order field: A defined part of the order record. / ambiguous entry: Information that permits more than one interpretation. / clarification request: A request to resolve an unclear or missing instruction. / current order: The order presently being processed, distinct from past records. / historical record: Information about a previous order or event. / verified instruction: A direction confirmed through the appropriate process.
Speaking task: Read the strength question aloud without guessing a number or naming a medicine. Complete the transfer with dosage form, the reported box description, the accepting pharmacist, and the actual timing status. Check the four answers, then repeat the handoff while keeping memory separate from a verified current instruction.
Partner: Ellis: Patient / Nia: Pharmacy technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A current order has an unclear dosage-form field. The patient remembers a white box. Pharmacist Ren accepts the clarification. No product form or completion time has been confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ellis: If I have a question about how to take the medicine once the order is clarified, should I ask Maya that as well?
Nia: Yes. Questions about the directions for use need the pharmacist's appropriate explanation. Resolving the strength field does not mean every question about your medicine has already been answered.
Ellis: Thank you. Please tell Maya that I remember a blue label and a usual pattern, but that I have not supplied a confirmed current strength.
Nia: I will preserve that in intake. The strength remains unclear, Maya is the clarification route, and no completion time is confirmed. We will not fill the gap with an assumption.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Scope: All cases, calculations, and product limits are fictional language exercises, not patient-specific directions or clinical certification. Follow local law, authorized duties, pharmacist direction, privacy procedures, and actual recall notices. Urgent concerns need the appropriate prompt response, not a routine update window. US and UK sources provide context, not interchangeable rules. This book does not teach prescribing, dose selection, or clinical counseling.
Lesson 01: Prescription intake without assumptions
Original fictional case: Patient Ellis asks technician Nia to complete a prescription order whose strength field is unclear. Ellis remembers a blue label and says the prescriber usually orders the same thing. Nia cannot select the strength. Pharmacist Maya can take the clarification, but no completion time is known. Nia must distinguish remembered information from verified current instructions, explain the unresolved field, and route the question. No medicine name, dose, or numerical strength is provided in this teaching case, and the exercise does not ask the learner to infer one.
Language workshop: A familiar label does not resolve an unclear strength
Communication goal: Identify an ambiguous order field and arrange pharmacist clarification without selecting a strength from memory or appearance.
Language guidance: Strength and dose are not synonyms. A product may contain a particular amount per unit, while the prescribed administration uses a specified amount and schedule. This unit supplies neither and does not authorize calculation or selection from a remembered label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The strength field on this order is unclear. / I understand that you usually receive the same medicine. / The label color is useful context, but it does not confirm the current strength. / I cannot select a strength from an unclear entry. / Pharmacist Maya can take the clarification. / We need the order clarified; I am not asking you to guess it. / I will record that you remember a blue label. / Strength describes the product; dose concerns the prescribed amount used. / We need the current instructions confirmed, not just an earlier pattern. / The pharmacist can review the relevant previous record. / I do not yet have a confirmed completion time. / The clarification is pending, not completed. / Can Maya confirm receipt of this question? / Any question about how to take it should go to the pharmacist. / Please tell the pharmacist about any concern that needs prompt attention. / We will use the verified instruction rather than fill the gap with a guess.
Vocabulary: prescription: An authorized order for a medicine under the applicable rules. / intake: Receiving and organizing information needed to process an order. / prescriber: A professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine. / pharmacist: A qualified medicines professional with the relevant practice authority. / strength: The amount of active ingredient in a stated unit or quantity of the product. / dose: The amount prescribed for a particular administration. / dosage form: The physical form of the product, such as a tablet or liquid. / active ingredient: The substance intended to produce the medicine's therapeutic effect. / concentration: The amount of a substance within a specified quantity of a preparation. / route: The specified way a medicine is taken or applied. / frequency: How often a prescribed action or administration occurs. / directions for use: The authorized instructions for taking or using the medicine. / quantity: The amount of product specified for supply. / days supply: Days represented by quantity and verified directions under the relevant calculation rules; not proof of actual use. / brand name: A trade name for a particular product. / generic name: The recognized nonproprietary name of the medicine substance. / product identifier: A code or reference identifying a specified product. / NDC: National Drug Code; a United States drug product identification system. / order field: A defined part of the order record. / ambiguous entry: Information that permits more than one interpretation. / clarification request: A request to resolve an unclear or missing instruction. / current order: The order presently being processed, distinct from past records. / historical record: Information about a previous order or event. / verified instruction: A direction confirmed through the appropriate process.
Speaking task: Read the strength question aloud without guessing a number or naming a medicine. Complete the transfer with dosage form, the reported box description, the accepting pharmacist, and the actual timing status. Check the four answers, then repeat the handoff while keeping memory separate from a verified current instruction.
Partner: Ellis: Patient / Nia: Pharmacy technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A current order has an unclear dosage-form field. The patient remembers a white box. Pharmacist Ren accepts the clarification. No product form or completion time has been confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ellis: If I have a question about how to take the medicine once the order is clarified, should I ask Maya that as well?
Nia: Yes. Questions about the directions for use need the pharmacist's appropriate explanation. Resolving the strength field does not mean every question about your medicine has already been answered.
Ellis: Thank you. Please tell Maya that I remember a blue label and a usual pattern, but that I have not supplied a confirmed current strength.
Nia: I will preserve that in intake. The strength remains unclear, Maya is the clarification route, and no completion time is confirmed. We will not fill the gap with an assumption.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE

02Insurance claims in plain EnglishPrior authorization required is not a final exclusion+

Your goal

Explain an insurance response accurately and connect the patient with an accepted status query without promising coverage.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Prior authorization required is not a final exclusion

Patient Morgan sees the response "prior authorization required" for a pharmacy claim and believes the medicine is permanently excluded. No authorization decision or accepted claim has arrived. Technician Alex can explain the recorded status without promising coverage, a price, or a deadline. Billing colleague Inez has accepted the status query and can explain the request route. The conversation should distinguish the plan requirement, any later decision, and the current pharmacy status. No particular insurance policy, appeal outcome, clinical alternative, or patient payment amount is established in the scenario.

Morgan: Patient / Alex: Pharmacy technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the current response say?

Answer and explanation

D. Prior authorization required

The response identifies a prior-authorization requirement rather than a final permanent exclusion.

2. What is still absent?

Answer and explanation

A. An authorization decision and an accepted claim

The brief explicitly leaves the decision and claim acceptance unconfirmed.

3. Who accepted the status query?

Answer and explanation

D. Inez

Inez is the identified billing colleague handling the query and explaining its route.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Prior authorization required states a plan requirement. It does not by itself establish permanent exclusion, approval, an accepted claim, or the final patient price. Explain the actual response and have the appropriate colleague clarify the applicable request route.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
prior authorization
Plan approval that may be required before specified care or a prescription is covered.request prior authorization
health plan
The insurance arrangement with defined benefits and rules.verify the health plan
claim
A request submitted for payment or benefit processing.submit a claim
claim response
The result or message returned for a submitted claim.explain the claim response
adjudication
The process of evaluating a claim under the applicable plan rules.check claim adjudication
response code
A coded message identifying a claim-processing result or requirement.clarify the response code
rejected claim
A claim not accepted as submitted under the returned response.investigate a rejected claim
accepted claim
A claim accepted under the relevant processing response.verify an accepted claim
coverage decision
The plan's determination about the relevant coverage request.confirm the coverage decision
coverage exclusion
A benefit or service not covered under the applicable plan terms.verify a coverage exclusion
formulary
A plan's list of medicines and their coverage-related conditions.check the formulary
benefit tier
A plan category that may affect coverage conditions and cost sharing.identify the benefit tier
copayment
A fixed patient payment for a covered service under the plan.verify the copayment
coinsurance
A patient share calculated as a percentage under the applicable benefit.explain coinsurance
deductible
The amount a person may need to pay before specified plan benefits apply.verify the deductible
out-of-pocket cost
The amount the person pays rather than the plan.confirm out-of-pocket cost
pharmacy network
Pharmacies participating under a plan's arrangements.verify pharmacy network status
PBM
Pharmacy benefit manager; an organization administering defined pharmacy-benefit functions.identify the relevant PBM
member identifier
The plan reference identifying the insured member.verify the member identifier
step therapy
A plan requirement to try specified treatment steps under its rules.clarify a step-therapy requirement
quantity limit
A plan or order restriction on the amount supplied under specified conditions.verify a quantity limit
exception request
A request for an exception under the plan's defined process.route an exception request
appeal
A request to review a relevant adverse decision through the applicable process.clarify the appeal route
status query
A request to establish the current stage, requirement, or result.accept a status query

A coverage decision and claim processing can involve different information and stages. Do not promise that one status automatically resolves every payment condition. Plan rules differ; use the actual response and verified benefit information rather than a rule remembered from another patient.

C Notice the language

Translate the message without softening its meaning

A short insurer response can sound like a permanent refusal. Explain the actual status in ordinary language while preserving what remains unknown. Avoid both a hopeless conclusion and reassurance that approval is merely a formality.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Read the status
The response says prior authorization required.
Explain the term
The plan is asking for its approval process to be completed.
Correct an inference
That message alone does not establish a permanent exclusion.
Avoid false reassurance
It also does not mean approval is guaranteed.
Name what is missing
We do not yet have an authorization decision or an accepted claim.
Identify the owner
Inez has accepted the status query.
Offer the route
Inez can explain how the request is handled for this plan.
Keep the price unconfirmed
I cannot confirm your final payment from this response alone.
Separate stages
A request sent is not a decision received.
Preserve the original response
We should keep the exact message in the query.
Clarify responsibility
Who is providing the information the plan needs?
Avoid a clinical substitution
Any medicine-alternative question needs the pharmacist.
Keep deadlines honest
No response deadline has been confirmed in this conversation.
Do not pre-judge an appeal
The relevant next process depends on the actual decision and plan rules.
Check understanding
The requirement is identified, but the outcome remains open.
Close with a useful next step
I will connect your question with Inez and the verified request route.
6 language and precision notes
That message alone
Limits the conclusion to what the available response establishes.
Also does not mean
Corrects overoptimism as well as an overly negative inference.
Has accepted
Names a real handoff owner rather than a vague future referral.
From this response alone
Explains why a final price cannot yet be promised.
Request sent versus decision received
Separates activity from outcome.
Depends on the actual decision
Avoids inventing the right appeal or exception process before the facts are known.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. The plan requires prior authorization; no decision or accepted claim has arrived.

The explanation preserves the actual requirement and open outcome without inventing permanent denial or approval.

2. Which statement overstates the billing handoff?

Answer and explanation

B. Inez accepted the query, so the claim is now approved.

Accepting a status query is an administrative action and does not establish a plan decision.

3. Which statement accurately separates the request from the result?

Answer and explanation

A. Submission does not establish that authorization was granted.

Submitting a request begins or advances a process but does not establish the result.

4. What should happen to a question about a different medicine?

Answer and explanation

D. Refer it to the pharmacist for appropriate clinical discussion.

A payment response does not authorize the technician to make a clinical substitution.

E Conversations

Prior authorization required is not a final exclusion

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Morgan: Patient / Alex: Pharmacy technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • appeal
  • prior authorization
  • claim response
  • accepted claim
  • out-of-pocket cost
  • coverage decision
  • copayment
  • health plan
  • status query
  • pharmacist
  1. Morgan
    Alex, this message says prior authorization required. Does that mean my medicine is excluded permanently and there is no point asking the plan anything else?
  2. Alex
    It identifies a 1 requirement, not a permanent exclusion by itself. We do not yet have the plan's authorization decision or an accepted claim, so the outcome remains open.
  3. Morgan
    My friend said you send a form and it goes through. Is that certain, or are we waiting for a real decision?
  4. Alex
    I cannot promise that 2. The request has to follow the actual plan process, and the result is not established merely because someone starts gathering or sending information.
  5. Morgan
    So it is neither a final no nor a definite yes. What is actually blocking the claim today?
  6. Alex
    We should preserve the exact 3 and explain its current meaning. It tells us what requirement was returned, but it does not confirm the final price, supply status, or a favorable decision.
  7. Morgan
    Who is looking into that requirement? I do not want my question to be passed from person to person without anyone knowing what happens next.
  8. Alex
    Inez has accepted the 4. She can explain the request route for this plan and identify the information needed, rather than leave you with a general instruction to contact somebody.
  9. Morgan
    If Inez speaks with the plan, will that automatically make the claim accepted, or could more information still be needed afterward?
  10. Alex
    An 5 is a separate status that needs verification. A telephone conversation or a submitted request does not, by itself, show that the claim has been accepted.
  11. Morgan
    I also need to know what I might pay. Can you read the current message and tell me my final amount now?
  12. Alex
    This response does not establish your 6. The applicable benefit information and claim result need to be checked, and I should not supply a number that the record does not support.
  13. Morgan
    My friend has the same medicine on a different plan and pays a small fixed amount. Does that tell us what my plan will do?
  14. Alex
    Their 7 does not establish yours. Plans and individual benefit circumstances can differ, so we need your actual plan information rather than copy another person's experience.
  15. Morgan
    Should I immediately appeal, then, or is that a different step from the requirement we are looking at now?
  16. Alex
    An 8 depends on the relevant decision and process. Inez can clarify the current route; I should not skip to an appeal or promise a particular outcome before that is established.
  17. Morgan
    If another medicine might be an option, I would like that question answered too. I do not want to make a medical decision from an insurance message.
  18. Alex
    The 9 should discuss any clinical alternative with you through the appropriate process. We can keep that question separate from Inez's work on the claim requirement.
  19. Morgan
    Please tell Inez that I understand the requirement is not a permanent exclusion, but I still need the route, outstanding information, and actual next step.
  20. Alex
    I will. The 10 process remains unresolved, no final charge is confirmed, and Inez owns the status query. We will explain verified developments without turning them into a guarantee.

Answers and explanations
  1. prior authorization Turn 2. Prior authorization is the plan approval requirement stated in the response, not a completed decision or permanent exclusion.
  2. coverage decision Turn 4. The coverage decision is the plan's actual determination, which has not arrived and cannot be assumed from submitting a form.
  3. claim response Turn 6. The claim response is the recorded message requiring prior authorization; it should be explained without changing its meaning.
  4. status query Turn 8. The status query has an identified owner, Inez, but accepting it does not resolve the coverage requirement.
  5. accepted claim Turn 10. An accepted claim is a processing result that must be verified separately from a colleague's inquiry or contact attempt.
  6. out-of-pocket cost Turn 12. Out-of-pocket cost is the patient's payment amount, which cannot be calculated or guaranteed from this requirement message alone.
  7. copayment Turn 14. A copayment is a fixed payment under a particular benefit, not evidence of another patient's coverage or charge.
  8. appeal Turn 16. An appeal reviews a relevant adverse decision under the applicable process; the current requirement does not establish that this is already the right step.
  9. pharmacist Turn 18. The pharmacist is the appropriate route for a clinical alternatives question; insurance processing does not authorize the technician to choose treatment.
  10. health plan Turn 20. The health plan's actual rules and decision govern the coverage process; the technician's explanation does not create approval or a deadline.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Morgan: Alex, this message says prior authorization required. Does that mean my medicine is excluded permanently and there is no point asking the plan anything else?
  2. Alex: It identifies a prior authorization requirement, not a permanent exclusion by itself. We do not yet have the plan's authorization decision or an accepted claim, so the outcome remains open.
  3. Morgan: My friend said you send a form and it goes through. Is that certain, or are we waiting for a real decision?
  4. Alex: I cannot promise that coverage decision. The request has to follow the actual plan process, and the result is not established merely because someone starts gathering or sending information.
  5. Morgan: So it is neither a final no nor a definite yes. What is actually blocking the claim today?
  6. Alex: We should preserve the exact claim response and explain its current meaning. It tells us what requirement was returned, but it does not confirm the final price, supply status, or a favorable decision.
  7. Morgan: Who is looking into that requirement? I do not want my question to be passed from person to person without anyone knowing what happens next.
  8. Alex: Inez has accepted the status query. She can explain the request route for this plan and identify the information needed, rather than leave you with a general instruction to contact somebody.
  9. Morgan: If Inez speaks with the plan, will that automatically make the claim accepted, or could more information still be needed afterward?
  10. Alex: An accepted claim is a separate status that needs verification. A telephone conversation or a submitted request does not, by itself, show that the claim has been accepted.
  11. Morgan: I also need to know what I might pay. Can you read the current message and tell me my final amount now?
  12. Alex: This response does not establish your out-of-pocket cost. The applicable benefit information and claim result need to be checked, and I should not supply a number that the record does not support.
  13. Morgan: My friend has the same medicine on a different plan and pays a small fixed amount. Does that tell us what my plan will do?
  14. Alex: Their copayment does not establish yours. Plans and individual benefit circumstances can differ, so we need your actual plan information rather than copy another person's experience.
  15. Morgan: Should I immediately appeal, then, or is that a different step from the requirement we are looking at now?
  16. Alex: An appeal depends on the relevant decision and process. Inez can clarify the current route; I should not skip to an appeal or promise a particular outcome before that is established.
  17. Morgan: If another medicine might be an option, I would like that question answered too. I do not want to make a medical decision from an insurance message.
  18. Alex: The pharmacist should discuss any clinical alternative with you through the appropriate process. We can keep that question separate from Inez's work on the claim requirement.
  19. Morgan: Please tell Inez that I understand the requirement is not a permanent exclusion, but I still need the route, outstanding information, and actual next step.
  20. Alex: I will. The health plan process remains unresolved, no final charge is confirmed, and Inez owns the status query. We will explain verified developments without turning them into a guarantee.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A requirement, not a final refusal

Fictional pharmacy counter using the prior-authorization case.

  1. Patient: The message says prior authorization required. Does that mean my plan will never cover this?
  2. Technician: This claim rejection requires a coverage review. We haven't received the plan's decision yet.
  3. Patient: So someone has approved it, but you haven't prepared the prescription for collection?
  4. Technician: No approval or accepted claim is showing. Preparation and collection are separate questions as well.
  5. Patient: Who can tell me what happens to the request? I keep hearing unfamiliar terms.
  6. Technician: Inez, our billing colleague, has accepted your query. She can explain the request route.
  7. Patient: Could she guarantee the plan will pay if the prescriber sends the information?
  8. Technician: No. Sending information and receiving approval are different stages; the decision belongs to the plan.
  9. Patient: Then please ask her where the request stands, not whether the medicine is ready.
  10. Technician: That's the question I'll pass on. The current response still says prior authorization required.
A friend's ten-dollar payment

Fictional accepted claim shows a twenty-dollar copayment; the patient mentions a friend's different plan.

  1. Patient: My friend pays ten dollars. Why does the amount on my receipt say twenty?
  2. Technician: Your current processed claim shows a twenty-dollar copayment. Your friend's amount doesn't establish yours.
  3. Patient: We use the same insurance company. I assumed that meant identical coverage for both of us.
  4. Technician: The company can administer different plans. A formulary listing can have different cost-sharing conditions.
  5. Patient: Is twenty an estimate, or is that what this particular claim actually returned?
  6. Technician: It's the amount returned for this claim. I can refer questions about how it was determined.
  7. Patient: I'd like that explanation before paying. I'm not asking you to choose a cheaper medicine.
  8. Technician: Understood. Inez can discuss the claim; medicine alternatives would be a question for the pharmacist.
  9. Patient: Could I speak to Inez away from the queue? I'd rather discuss the details privately.
  10. Technician: Yes. The consultation counter is free, and I'll tell her you're asking about this claim's copayment.
The number on an old card

Fictional billing call: claim rejected because member information needs verification; acceptance is not guaranteed.

  1. Technician: This claim was rejected with a member-information message. The patient brought an older insurance card.
  2. Billing colleague: Does the profile match that card, or have you verified information for the current plan year?
  3. Technician: It matches the older card. The patient says a replacement arrived but hasn't shown it yet.
  4. Billing colleague: Then our immediate question is which membership information is current, not whether coverage is permanently excluded.
  5. Technician: Can you review the current card with the patient when they bring it this afternoon?
  6. Billing colleague: Yes. I can check those details and handle the claim query once the information is available.
  7. Technician: Should I promise acceptance after we update the number?
  8. Billing colleague: No, updating information doesn't guarantee acceptance. We need the response to the revised submission.
  9. Technician: I'll explain that today's rejection concerns this submission and that your review is the next step.
  10. Billing colleague: Please also ask them to bring the current card; that gives our conversation a concrete starting point.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Prior authorization required is not a final exclusion
  1. Read the prior-authorization exchange with requirement, decision, and accepted claim clearly separated.
  2. Complete the quantity-limit transfer using its named billing colleague and pharmacist route.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Explain a different unresolved claim requirement

A response says quantity limit exceeded. No exception decision or accepted claim is available. Billing colleague Sam accepts the query; the pharmacist handles questions about changing treatment.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: "The returned requirement concerns a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. quantity limit

The response specifically identifies a quantity limit rather than a completed exception decision.

2. Patient: "The billing query is with ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Sam

Sam is the colleague who accepted the status query.

3. Technician: "Any clinical change should be discussed with the ___."

Answer and explanation

B. pharmacist

The supplied role assigns treatment questions to the pharmacist, not the billing response.

4. Technician: "The exception outcome remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unconfirmed

No exception decision or accepted claim has arrived, so the outcome is still open.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. REFERENCE
Course: Pharmacy Technician English
Professional audience: Pharmacy technicians and pharmacy support staff
Scope: All cases, calculations, and product limits are fictional language exercises, not patient-specific directions or clinical certification. Follow local law, authorized duties, pharmacist direction, privacy procedures, and actual recall notices. Urgent concerns need the appropriate prompt response, not a routine update window. US and UK sources provide context, not interchangeable rules. This book does not teach prescribing, dose selection, or clinical counseling.
Lesson 02: Insurance claims in plain English
Original fictional case: Patient Morgan sees the response "prior authorization required" for a pharmacy claim and believes the medicine is permanently excluded. No authorization decision or accepted claim has arrived. Technician Alex can explain the recorded status without promising coverage, a price, or a deadline. Billing colleague Inez has accepted the status query and can explain the request route. The conversation should distinguish the plan requirement, any later decision, and the current pharmacy status. No particular insurance policy, appeal outcome, clinical alternative, or patient payment amount is established in the scenario.
Language workshop: Prior authorization required is not a final exclusion
Communication goal: Explain an insurance response accurately and connect the patient with an accepted status query without promising coverage.
Language guidance: Prior authorization required states a plan requirement. It does not by itself establish permanent exclusion, approval, an accepted claim, or the final patient price. Explain the actual response and have the appropriate colleague clarify the applicable request route.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The response says prior authorization required. / The plan is asking for its approval process to be completed. / That message alone does not establish a permanent exclusion. / It also does not mean approval is guaranteed. / We do not yet have an authorization decision or an accepted claim. / Inez has accepted the status query. / Inez can explain how the request is handled for this plan. / I cannot confirm your final payment from this response alone. / A request sent is not a decision received. / We should keep the exact message in the query. / Who is providing the information the plan needs? / Any medicine-alternative question needs the pharmacist. / No response deadline has been confirmed in this conversation. / The relevant next process depends on the actual decision and plan rules. / The requirement is identified, but the outcome remains open. / I will connect your question with Inez and the verified request route.
Vocabulary: prior authorization: Plan approval that may be required before specified care or a prescription is covered. / health plan: The insurance arrangement with defined benefits and rules. / claim: A request submitted for payment or benefit processing. / claim response: The result or message returned for a submitted claim. / adjudication: The process of evaluating a claim under the applicable plan rules. / response code: A coded message identifying a claim-processing result or requirement. / rejected claim: A claim not accepted as submitted under the returned response. / accepted claim: A claim accepted under the relevant processing response. / coverage decision: The plan's determination about the relevant coverage request. / coverage exclusion: A benefit or service not covered under the applicable plan terms. / formulary: A plan's list of medicines and their coverage-related conditions. / benefit tier: A plan category that may affect coverage conditions and cost sharing. / copayment: A fixed patient payment for a covered service under the plan. / coinsurance: A patient share calculated as a percentage under the applicable benefit. / deductible: The amount a person may need to pay before specified plan benefits apply. / out-of-pocket cost: The amount the person pays rather than the plan. / pharmacy network: Pharmacies participating under a plan's arrangements. / PBM: Pharmacy benefit manager; an organization administering defined pharmacy-benefit functions. / member identifier: The plan reference identifying the insured member. / step therapy: A plan requirement to try specified treatment steps under its rules. / quantity limit: A plan or order restriction on the amount supplied under specified conditions. / exception request: A request for an exception under the plan's defined process. / appeal: A request to review a relevant adverse decision through the applicable process. / status query: A request to establish the current stage, requirement, or result.
Speaking task: Read the prior-authorization exchange with requirement, decision, and accepted claim clearly separated. Complete the quantity-limit transfer using its named billing colleague and pharmacist route. Check the key, then repeat the final status without promising either a payment amount or an exception decision.
Partner: Morgan: Patient / Alex: Pharmacy technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A response says quantity limit exceeded. No exception decision or accepted claim is available. Billing colleague Sam accepts the query; the pharmacist handles questions about changing treatment.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Morgan: If another medicine might be an option, I would like that question answered too. I do not want to make a medical decision from an insurance message.
Alex: The pharmacist should discuss any clinical alternative with you through the appropriate process. We can keep that question separate from Inez's work on the claim requirement.
Morgan: Please tell Inez that I understand the requirement is not a permanent exclusion, but I still need the route, outstanding information, and actual next step.
Alex: I will. The health plan process remains unresolved, no final charge is confirmed, and Inez owns the status query. We will explain verified developments without turning them into a guarantee.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
