Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A question before collection

Fictional pickup counter using the label-handoff case.

  1. Technician: You mentioned the auxiliary label, the extra label here. Which part would you like explained?
  2. Patient: I recognize the main instructions, but I don't understand what this additional warning means.
  3. Technician: Ravi, our pharmacist, can explain it at the consultation counter. I won't interpret the instruction.
  4. Patient: Could you tell him I'm asking about this label, not asking for a different medicine?
  5. Technician: Yes, I'll pass on that distinction. He has accepted the consultation and is waiting there.
  6. Patient: I feel awkward holding up the queue when everyone else seems ready to pay.
  7. Technician: The consultation counter gives you space for the question without having to discuss it here.
  8. Patient: Does talking to Ravi mean my instructions have changed, or will he explain this label?
  9. Technician: No instructions have changed. Ravi's counseling conversation will explain the label already attached.
  10. Patient: Then I'll speak with Ravi first. Please keep my collection question separate from the label discussion.
Tablet is not a strength

Fictional patient uses the word strength when asking about tablet versus liquid.

  1. Patient: I wanted the other strength, the liquid one. Is that what the prescription says?
  2. Technician: Do you mean the physical form, liquid instead of tablet, rather than the ingredient amount?
  3. Patient: Yes, I meant the form. I sometimes use the wrong word at the counter.
  4. Technician: That's called the dosage form. The current order lists tablets; I'll pass your question to the pharmacist.
  5. Patient: Could the pharmacist discuss my concern without you changing the order before that conversation?
  6. Technician: Certainly. I've identified the wording difference, but haven't requested or selected a replacement medicine.
  7. Patient: Please mention that I want to discuss the form, not increase or decrease anything.
  8. Technician: I'll use those words in the handoff. They make your request much clearer.
  9. Patient: Thank you. I was worried that saying other strength had already changed the record.
  10. Technician: It hasn't changed the order. I'll flag the form question for the pharmacist's discussion with you.
Two bags, two statuses

Fictional patient has one prescription ready and another awaiting pharmacist review.

  1. Patient: I received a ready message. Does that cover both prescriptions I asked about yesterday?
  2. Technician: The notice covers one prescription. The second prescription's dispensing status remains pharmacist review pending.
  3. Patient: Can you show me which reference is ready without saying the medicine names aloud?
  4. Technician: Yes, let's check the references here privately. The notice matches the number ending in eighteen.
  5. Patient: The other ends in twenty-three. I had assumed the message included everything under my name.
  6. Technician: It doesn't. I can confirm eighteen is ready; twenty-three has a separate pending status.
  7. Patient: I have a question about the pending one. Is the counter queue the right place?
  8. Technician: I'll ask the pharmacist to discuss that question with you at the consultation counter.
  9. Patient: Please don't mark both collected just because I've arrived for the first one.
  10. Technician: Understood. Each prescription retains its own status, and today's notice only confirms readiness for eighteen.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 05: Pickup questions and pharmacist consultations
Original fictional case: At pickup, patient Arden asks technician Kit what an auxiliary label means. Kit can identify the label but is not authorized to interpret its instruction. Pharmacist Ravi is available at the consultation counter and has accepted the question. No instructions are changed in this scenario, and the actual label wording is not supplied as a clinical direction. Kit should acknowledge the uncertainty, explain the handoff, and help Arden reach Ravi without implying that a label is optional or that handing over the package has already answered the question.
Language workshop: The label question has a named pharmacist
Communication goal: Recognize an auxiliary-label question and complete a clear pharmacist handoff without interpreting or changing the instruction.
Language guidance: An auxiliary label adds information; it is not optional merely because it is separate from the main label. The technician can identify which label prompted the question while the pharmacist explains its meaning for the actual medicine and patient.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for asking about that label. / You are referring to the auxiliary label beside the main dispensing label. / I can identify it, but I cannot interpret the instruction for you. / Ravi has accepted your question. / Ravi is available at the consultation counter. / I will pass on the exact label and your question. / An additional label is not automatically optional. / I am not changing any direction on this package. / The pharmacist will discuss what the instruction means for your medicine. / We can use the practice's appropriate private discussion arrangement. / Please tell Ravi which part is unclear. / You can explain what you understood and ask Ravi to clarify it. / The question is accepted; the consultation has not yet been completed. / Ravi can address the appropriate use of the supplied device within the consultation. / Handing over a package does not itself answer a clinical question. / I will connect you with Ravi, who has accepted the question.
Vocabulary: auxiliary label: An additional label providing a relevant medicine instruction or caution. / dispensing label: The pharmacy label with the relevant patient and medicine directions. / patient counseling: A professional discussion helping the patient understand the medicine and its use. / consultation counter: The designated place for the pharmacist discussion. / medication guide: Patient information supplied for certain medicines under relevant requirements. / patient information leaflet: Written product information intended for the patient. / package insert: Product information supplied with the medicine, with audience and purpose varying by format. / warning: Information drawing attention to a relevant risk or required caution. / precaution: A condition or measure requiring care in using a medicine. / contraindication: A condition in which a medicine or treatment should not be used under its labeling or clinical assessment. / interaction: A relevant effect arising from a medicine used with another substance or condition. / food interaction: A possible effect involving medicine use and food. / alcohol interaction: A possible effect involving medicine use and alcohol. / drowsiness: A tendency to feel sleepy or less alert. / storage condition: A requirement for keeping a product appropriately. / refrigeration: Storage under specified refrigerated conditions when required. / light protection: Storage or handling intended to limit relevant light exposure. / measuring device: A device intended to measure an appropriate quantity accurately. / administration device: Equipment used to deliver a medicine under the specified directions. / privacy: Protection of personal information and conversation from inappropriate exposure. / teach-back: A check in which the person explains their understanding so the professional can clarify it. / accepted handoff: A transfer of a question or task acknowledged by the receiving person. / instruction interpretation: Explanation of what a direction means for the person's medicine use. / counseling completion: Confirmation that the actual professional discussion occurred, not merely that it was offered.
Speaking task: Read the label question and named pharmacist handoff without adding a medicine instruction. Complete the storage transfer with Noor and the consultation area. Check all four answers, then repeat that the direction is unchanged and the consultation has not yet happened.
Partner: Arden: Patient / Kit: Pharmacy technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Patient Drew asks about storage wording. The technician cannot interpret it. Pharmacist Noor accepts the question and is available in the consultation area. No instruction is changed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Arden: Once Ravi explains it, I may need to repeat what I understood. Sometimes I say yes because I heard the words, but the meaning is still unclear.
Kit: That kind of teach-back can help Ravi clarify the explanation. You can say what you understood and ask about anything that does not fit, rather than feel obliged to pretend.
Arden: Thank you. Please keep the original directions unchanged and connect me with Ravi. I want the meaning clarified, not a new instruction guessed at this counter.
Kit: No direction has been changed. We will distinguish the handoff from counseling completion, and Ravi will address the question through the consultation. I will preserve the label reference and your request for a clear explanation.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Private collection and contact preferencesA neighbor at the counter+

Your goal

Explain a private verification step without accusing the collector or disclosing a patient medication list.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A neighbor at the counter

Pavel asks to collect a prescription for a neighbor. Technician Vera has not yet verified the collection arrangement under the pharmacy process. Pavel asks Vera to read the neighbor's medicines aloud so he can recognize the correct package. Lead pharmacist Maya is available to review the request privately. No pickup decision or contact permission has been established. The task is not to announce a universal ban on friends collecting prescriptions. Vera must protect information, avoid an improvised identity test, and give the request a clear next step.

Pavel: Neighbor requesting collection / Vera: Pharmacy technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is unresolved?

Answer and explanation

D. The collection arrangement under the pharmacy process

The case establishes an unverified arrangement, not wrongdoing or a universal prohibition.

2. Who can review the request privately?

Answer and explanation

A. Lead pharmacist Maya

Maya is the available pharmacist identified to review this request.

3. Which requested action should Vera avoid?

Answer and explanation

C. Reading the medication list aloud as an improvised identification test

Naming medicines publicly would reveal information without resolving the collection arrangement through the appropriate process.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Identity, relationship, and authority answer different questions. Knowing a person is a neighbor does not by itself settle every disclosure. Nor does uncertainty create a universal ban on third-party collection. Apply the actual pharmacy process and qualified judgment.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
collection arrangement
The agreed or permitted process for another person to collect an item.verify the collection arrangement
designated collector
Person identified to collect on someone else's behalf.confirm the designated collector
identity verification
Checking a person's identity using the applicable process.complete identity verification
authority to collect
Basis on which a person may receive another person's prescription.clarify authority to collect
professional judgment
Qualified assessment using relevant facts and applicable standards.exercise professional judgment
reasonable inference
Conclusion supported by circumstances rather than certainty from a single claim.make a reasonable inference
disclosure
Making information available to another person.limit the disclosure
confidentiality
Duty to protect information from inappropriate access or sharing.maintain confidentiality
patient preference
A person's expressed choice about care or communication.record a patient preference
contact permission
Established basis for contacting or sharing through a particular person or channel.verify contact permission
preferred channel
Communication method the patient has chosen where appropriate.confirm the preferred channel
alternate contact
Another person or destination used when authorized and suitable.verify an alternate contact
voicemail
Recorded message left when a call is unanswered.check voicemail preferences
callback number
Number to use for a return call after appropriate verification.verify the callback number
relationship claim
Statement that someone has a particular connection to a patient.assess the relationship claim
pickup request
Request to receive a prepared prescription from the pharmacy.review a pickup request
private review
Assessment conducted with appropriate protection from public hearing.arrange a private review
public counter
Customer-facing space where other people may hear a conversation.avoid details at the public counter
medication list
Record of medicines associated with a patient.protect the medication list
relevant information
Details needed for the specific permitted purpose.share relevant information
identity mismatch
Difference between identifying details that requires clarification.resolve an identity mismatch
authorization record
Documentation relevant to permission for a specified action.check an authorization record
third party
Person or organization other than the main parties to a matter.verify a third-party request
verified destination
Contact address or number checked through the appropriate process.use a verified destination

US health-privacy guidance allows professional judgment in appropriate friend or family prescription pickups; it does not impose advance written authorization in every case. This exercise concerns a still-unresolved request, not a new legal rule for every pharmacy or country.

C Notice the language

Explain the process without implying wrongdoing

A verification question can sound like an accusation when a collector believes they are helping. Acknowledge that purpose, identify the unresolved arrangement, and name the person who can review it. Privacy is not a reason to shame someone publicly.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the purpose
I understand that you have come to help your neighbor.
Name the unresolved step
We have not yet verified the collection arrangement.
Avoid an accusation
I am checking the arrangement, not accusing you of anything.
Protect the list
I cannot use a public reading of the medication list as an identification test.
Offer the next step
Maya can review the request privately.
Avoid a blanket ban
I am not saying friends can never collect prescriptions.
Separate identity and authority
Confirming who you are does not answer every collection question.
Keep the patient central
We need to handle the patient's information through the appropriate process.
Check the channel
We have not established permission to use that contact method.
Do not improvise a test
Please let us follow the pharmacy's verification process.
Limit the conversation
We can discuss the request without announcing medicine names here.
Clarify the decision status
A collection decision has not yet been made.
Refer the judgment
Maya can assess the relevant circumstances.
Protect contact details
I will not read out another person's contact information.
Make the handoff concrete
I will connect you with Maya and explain the unresolved collection request.
Close courteously
Thank you for waiting while we resolve this privately.
6 language and precision notes
Have not yet
States an unresolved step rather than a permanent refusal.
Not accusing
Repairs an interpersonal misunderstanding without abandoning verification.
As an identification test
Explains why the proposed disclosure is inappropriate.
Can review
Offers a practical next step without promising approval.
Not saying friends can never
Avoids turning a local uncertainty into a universal legal claim.
Relevant circumstances
Leaves professional judgment tied to actual evidence.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Pavel says, "So friends are never allowed to collect?" Which response is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. That is not what I am saying; Maya can review this unresolved request privately.

The response distinguishes this unresolved request from a universal rule and gives a confirmed review route.

2. Why does confirming Pavel's identity not finish this review?

Answer and explanation

B. It establishes who he is, while this collection arrangement still needs review.

Identity alone does not determine every collection or disclosure decision.

3. What can Vera promise now?

Answer and explanation

C. Connection with Maya for private review

Maya is available to review; the decision and contact permissions remain unestablished.

4. Which note is factual?

Answer and explanation

A. Neighbor collection requested; arrangement unresolved; referred to Maya privately.

The note records established events without inventing wrongdoing, legal requirements, or patient permission.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

A neighbor at the counter

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Pavel: Neighbor requesting collection / Vera: Pharmacy technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • relevant information
  • professional judgment
  • confidentiality
  • contact permission
  • pickup request
  • collection arrangement
  • authority to collect
  • identity verification
  • medication list
  • private review
  1. Pavel
    I am collecting for my neighbor. She asked me to stop here on my way home, but I do not know which package I need.
  2. Vera
    I understand. We need to verify the 1 through our process. I can ask lead pharmacist Maya to review your request privately.
  3. Pavel
    Could you just read out her medicines? I will probably recognize one of the names, and that would save us both some time.
  4. Vera
    I cannot use the 2 as a public identification test. We can take the request forward without announcing those names to everyone at the counter.
  5. Pavel
    Do you think I am lying? She asked me to help, and now I feel as though I have done something wrong.
  6. Vera
    No. I am not accusing you. The 3 needs review, and Maya is available to handle it with you. I appreciate why you have come.
  7. Pavel
    Does that mean friends and neighbors are never allowed to collect, or just that you have not settled the arrangement for this visit?
  8. Vera
    It means this request is unresolved. I am not announcing a blanket ban. Maya can use the applicable process and 4 in reviewing it.
  9. Pavel
    I can tell you my name and explain the relationship. Is proving who I am the same thing as proving that I can collect?
  10. Vera
    Those are related but distinct. 5 addresses who you are. The appropriate review also needs to resolve the collection arrangement without using medicine names as a guessing game.
  11. Pavel
    Would calling her help? I have a number on my phone, but I do not know what contact details or preferences your pharmacy holds.
  12. Vera
    We should use the appropriate process for 6 and verified details. I will not assume that every number or channel offered at the counter is suitable.
  13. Pavel
    Can we move away from the queue? I can explain why she could not come, but I would rather not announce it here.
  14. Vera
    Yes. Maya can provide a 7. I will pass on the collection question without describing your neighbor's medicines or circumstances to the waiting customers.
  15. Pavel
    Please make sure she knows that I am here to collect, rather than telling her that I asked for permission to see every record.
  16. Vera
    I will keep the 8 accurate: you requested collection for your neighbor, and we have not yet resolved the arrangement. That is the issue Maya will receive.
  17. Pavel
    Thank you. I can wait for that conversation. I mainly needed to know whether there was a next step instead of simply being turned away.
  18. Vera
    There is. We will protect 9 while Maya reviews it. I cannot promise the outcome before that review, but I can connect you with her.
  19. Pavel
    Then please do that. I understand that you have not approved or refused the collection yet and that we should discuss the details privately.
  20. Vera
    Exactly. The 10 will be addressed through the appropriate review. I will explain where the request stands and introduce you to Maya.

Answers and explanations
  1. collection arrangement Turn 2. The collection arrangement remains unresolved, so Vera names the process needing review rather than inventing approval or misconduct.
  2. medication list Turn 4. The medication list contains patient information and should not be read publicly as an improvised identity test.
  3. pickup request Turn 6. A pickup request records the action Pavel wants; its existence does not establish that the collection decision is complete.
  4. professional judgment Turn 8. Professional judgment applies relevant circumstances and rules; uncertainty does not justify inventing a universal ban on friend pickups.
  5. identity verification Turn 10. Identity verification concerns who Pavel is; it does not independently resolve every question about receiving another person's prescription.
  6. contact permission Turn 12. Contact permission must be established through the actual process rather than assumed from a number offered by another person.
  7. private review Turn 14. A private review moves the unresolved request to an appropriate setting without promising that collection will be approved.
  8. relevant information Turn 16. Relevant information is limited to the actual request and unresolved arrangement, not an invented demand for unrestricted patient records.
  9. confidentiality Turn 18. Confidentiality requires appropriate protection of information while the request follows its proper review route, not public accusation or disclosure.
  10. authority to collect Turn 20. Authority to collect is the unresolved basis for receiving the prescription; it must not be assumed from the request alone.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Pavel: I am collecting for my neighbor. She asked me to stop here on my way home, but I do not know which package I need.
  2. Vera: I understand. We need to verify the collection arrangement through our process. I can ask lead pharmacist Maya to review your request privately.
  3. Pavel: Could you just read out her medicines? I will probably recognize one of the names, and that would save us both some time.
  4. Vera: I cannot use the medication list as a public identification test. We can take the request forward without announcing those names to everyone at the counter.
  5. Pavel: Do you think I am lying? She asked me to help, and now I feel as though I have done something wrong.
  6. Vera: No. I am not accusing you. The pickup request needs review, and Maya is available to handle it with you. I appreciate why you have come.
  7. Pavel: Does that mean friends and neighbors are never allowed to collect, or just that you have not settled the arrangement for this visit?
  8. Vera: It means this request is unresolved. I am not announcing a blanket ban. Maya can use the applicable process and professional judgment in reviewing it.
  9. Pavel: I can tell you my name and explain the relationship. Is proving who I am the same thing as proving that I can collect?
  10. Vera: Those are related but distinct. identity verification addresses who you are. The appropriate review also needs to resolve the collection arrangement without using medicine names as a guessing game.
  11. Pavel: Would calling her help? I have a number on my phone, but I do not know what contact details or preferences your pharmacy holds.
  12. Vera: We should use the appropriate process for contact permission and verified details. I will not assume that every number or channel offered at the counter is suitable.
  13. Pavel: Can we move away from the queue? I can explain why she could not come, but I would rather not announce it here.
  14. Vera: Yes. Maya can provide a private review. I will pass on the collection question without describing your neighbor's medicines or circumstances to the waiting customers.
  15. Pavel: Please make sure she knows that I am here to collect, rather than telling her that I asked for permission to see every record.
  16. Vera: I will keep the relevant information accurate: you requested collection for your neighbor, and we have not yet resolved the arrangement. That is the issue Maya will receive.
  17. Pavel: Thank you. I can wait for that conversation. I mainly needed to know whether there was a next step instead of simply being turned away.
  18. Vera: There is. We will protect confidentiality while Maya reviews it. I cannot promise the outcome before that review, but I can connect you with her.
  19. Pavel: Then please do that. I understand that you have not approved or refused the collection yet and that we should discuss the details privately.
  20. Vera: Exactly. The authority to collect will be addressed through the appropriate review. I will explain where the request stands and introduce you to Maya.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Collecting for a neighbor

Fictional counter enquiry using the lesson privacy case.

  1. Visitor: I'm collecting for my neighbor. Read me the medicine names and I'll recognize the right bag.
  2. Technician: I can help with collection, but I can't read those details before the arrangements are verified.
  3. Visitor: She trusts me with her shopping, and I've helped her collect things here before.
  4. Technician: I appreciate that. We still need to verify today's arrangement before treating you as an authorized representative.
  5. Visitor: Are you saying you think I'm lying about knowing her or helping her?
  6. Technician: No. I'm explaining the information boundary, not questioning your intentions or your relationship.
  7. Visitor: Who can check it without making me explain everything in front of this queue?
  8. Technician: Maya can review the collection question privately. I haven't confirmed any package or medicine details.
  9. Visitor: I'll speak with her. Please tell her I'm here on my neighbor's behalf.
  10. Technician: I will. She can establish the relevant collection arrangements without discussing the neighbor's medicines across the counter.
A shared telephone

Fictional patient updates contact preferences; a shared home telephone should receive neutral messages only.

  1. Technician: You asked us to review your contact preference. Is the home telephone still suitable?
  2. Patient: Yes, but several people answer it. Please don't leave medicine names in a message.
  3. Technician: Would you prefer a neutral request to call the pharmacy back on that number?
  4. Patient: Yes. I can return a call, but I don't want the household hearing prescription details.
  5. Technician: I'll record that preference through our profile update process. Is your mobile number still current?
  6. Patient: It is, though I don't check it during work. The home number is more reliable.
  7. Technician: Then I'll keep the number and the message preference distinct; changing one doesn't require changing both.
  8. Patient: Could you repeat what you've understood before we finish? I want the instruction to be clear.
  9. Technician: Use the home number for a neutral callback request, without medicine names.
  10. Patient: That's exactly what I meant. Please let the team see that preference when they contact me.
A louder voice would not help

Fictional patient requests a quieter counter instead of having details repeated publicly.

  1. Patient: I didn't hear the question, but please don't repeat my details louder across the counter.
  2. Technician: Of course. The side counter is free; we can continue there with more privacy.
  3. Patient: Thank you. I have difficulty hearing when several conversations are happening at once.
  4. Technician: Would speaking clearly at the quieter counter help, or would you prefer another available communication method?
  5. Patient: The quieter space should help. I just need you to face me when speaking.
  6. Technician: We'll continue identity verification there without announcing private information. Confidentiality matters throughout the conversation.
  7. Patient: Will moving aside mean I lose my place and need to join the queue again?
  8. Technician: No, I'm continuing your enquiry with you. We are simply changing where the conversation happens.
  9. Patient: That's reassuring. I didn't want to choose between hearing the question and keeping things private.
  10. Technician: Let's move now. I'll repeat the question clearly once we're at the side counter.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A neighbor at the counter
  1. Read the private-collection exchange, keeping the courteous acknowledgment and unresolved arrangement together.
  2. Complete the alternate-number transfer without assuming that a supplied number establishes contact permission.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify an alternate contact request

Collector Sam supplies a new telephone number. Contact permission is unverified. Pharmacist Ellis accepts the private review. No patient information has been disclosed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: "The new ___ has not been verified."

Answer and explanation

A. telephone number

The supplied number needs verification through the actual contact process.

2. Collector: "Contact permission is still ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unverified

The facts do not establish permission to use this contact.

3. Technician: "Pharmacist ___ has accepted the review."

Answer and explanation

D. Ellis

Ellis is the pharmacist explicitly identified as the receiving owner.

4. Technician: "No patient information has been ___."

Answer and explanation

C. disclosed

The scenario states that patient information has not been disclosed.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Lesson 06: Private collection and contact preferences
Original fictional case: Pavel asks to collect a prescription for a neighbor. Technician Vera has not yet verified the collection arrangement under the pharmacy process. Pavel asks Vera to read the neighbor's medicines aloud so he can recognize the correct package. Lead pharmacist Maya is available to review the request privately. No pickup decision or contact permission has been established. The task is not to announce a universal ban on friends collecting prescriptions. Vera must protect information, avoid an improvised identity test, and give the request a clear next step.
Language workshop: A neighbor at the counter
Communication goal: Explain a private verification step without accusing the collector or disclosing a patient medication list.
Language guidance: Identity, relationship, and authority answer different questions. Knowing a person is a neighbor does not by itself settle every disclosure. Nor does uncertainty create a universal ban on third-party collection. Apply the actual pharmacy process and qualified judgment.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that you have come to help your neighbor. / We have not yet verified the collection arrangement. / I am checking the arrangement, not accusing you of anything. / I cannot use a public reading of the medication list as an identification test. / Maya can review the request privately. / I am not saying friends can never collect prescriptions. / Confirming who you are does not answer every collection question. / We need to handle the patient's information through the appropriate process. / We have not established permission to use that contact method. / Please let us follow the pharmacy's verification process. / We can discuss the request without announcing medicine names here. / A collection decision has not yet been made. / Maya can assess the relevant circumstances. / I will not read out another person's contact information. / I will connect you with Maya and explain the unresolved collection request. / Thank you for waiting while we resolve this privately.
Vocabulary: collection arrangement: The agreed or permitted process for another person to collect an item. / designated collector: Person identified to collect on someone else's behalf. / identity verification: Checking a person's identity using the applicable process. / authority to collect: Basis on which a person may receive another person's prescription. / professional judgment: Qualified assessment using relevant facts and applicable standards. / reasonable inference: Conclusion supported by circumstances rather than certainty from a single claim. / disclosure: Making information available to another person. / confidentiality: Duty to protect information from inappropriate access or sharing. / patient preference: A person's expressed choice about care or communication. / contact permission: Established basis for contacting or sharing through a particular person or channel. / preferred channel: Communication method the patient has chosen where appropriate. / alternate contact: Another person or destination used when authorized and suitable. / voicemail: Recorded message left when a call is unanswered. / callback number: Number to use for a return call after appropriate verification. / relationship claim: Statement that someone has a particular connection to a patient. / pickup request: Request to receive a prepared prescription from the pharmacy. / private review: Assessment conducted with appropriate protection from public hearing. / public counter: Customer-facing space where other people may hear a conversation. / medication list: Record of medicines associated with a patient. / relevant information: Details needed for the specific permitted purpose. / identity mismatch: Difference between identifying details that requires clarification. / authorization record: Documentation relevant to permission for a specified action. / third party: Person or organization other than the main parties to a matter. / verified destination: Contact address or number checked through the appropriate process.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Precise inventory and quality queriesAB41 is not AB14+

Your goal

Read back product identifiers and distinguish an unverified stock match from a confirmed recall assessment.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

AB41 is not AB14

A fictional recall notice names lot AB41. The delivery record shows AB14, but nobody has checked the label on the stock itself. Technician Emil brings the discrepancy to pharmacist Maya, who owns the recall assessment. The team must compare the actual product and lot with the complete notice through its approved process. Neither affected-stock status nor patient exposure has been established. A different number in one record does not prove the shelf is clear. No disposal, release, patient-contact, or treatment decision is supplied.

Emil: Pharmacy technician / Maya: Lead pharmacist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which source has not been checked?

Answer and explanation

C. The label on the actual stock

The case explicitly states that the stock label has not been checked.

2. What can be concluded now?

Answer and explanation

A. A discrepancy needs assessment; affected-stock status is not established.

The two records differ, but actual stock and full notice scope still need review.

3. Who owns the recall assessment?

Answer and explanation

C. Pharmacist Maya

Maya is the named professional responsible for this assessment.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Lot, serial, and product identifiers are not interchangeable. A product code identifies the product category or configuration; a lot identifies a production group. The complete recall notice determines which details matter. A matching fragment is not the full assessment.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
recall notice
Formal communication identifying a product problem and applicable actions.review the recall notice
lot number
Identifier for a defined production group.read back the lot number
batch
Defined quantity produced under a specified manufacturing process.trace the batch
serial number
Identifier assigned to a particular individual item or package.verify a serial number
product identifier
Code or set of details identifying a product.match the product identifier
National Drug Code
US identifier commonly abbreviated NDC for a listed drug product.compare the National Drug Code
manufacturer
Organization responsible for making a product.confirm the manufacturer
expiration date
Date after which a product should not be used under its labeling.check the expiration date
delivery record
Documentation of goods delivered to a location.reconcile the delivery record
physical stock
Actual inventory present rather than entries in a system.inspect the physical stock
stock reconciliation
Comparison of records with actual inventory to resolve differences.complete stock reconciliation
traceability
Ability to follow product history and movement through records.preserve traceability
quarantine
Controlled segregation or status preventing use pending an authorized decision.apply the quarantine process
affected stock
Inventory confirmed to fall within the relevant notice or problem scope.identify affected stock
recall scope
Defined products, lots, dates, or other limits of a recall.establish the recall scope
disposition
Authorized decision about what happens to a product.record the disposition
return authorization
Permission or reference required for an approved product return.obtain return authorization
destruction record
Documentation that product was destroyed through the authorized process.retain a destruction record
release decision
Authorized decision allowing stock to become available for its intended use.document a release decision
patient exposure
Situation in which a patient received or used a relevant product.assess patient exposure
first-expiry-first-out
Using the earliest-expiring eligible stock first; FEFO does not permit expired or quarantined stock.apply first-expiry-first-out
temperature excursion
Temperature outside specified storage limits.report a temperature excursion
tamper evidence
Visible feature intended to show that packaging may have been interfered with.check tamper evidence
quality defect
Product problem affecting conformity with relevant quality requirements.report a quality defect

Quarantine, return, destruction, and release are different controlled actions. Use the actual notice and pharmacy procedures, not this language exercise as an operational instruction. Questions about a patient taking medicine belong with the appropriate professional; a recall is not universal advice to stop treatment.

C Notice the language

Read identifiers, not impressions

A familiar-looking code can be misheard when colleagues are busy. Read the exact sequence, name the source, and ask for a read-back. Correcting AB41 to AB14 is a factual distinction, not a criticism of the other person.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the document
The notice names lot AB41.
Read the second source
The delivery record shows AB14.
Read back precisely
That is A, B, four, one on the notice.
State the missing check
The actual stock label has not yet been checked.
Avoid premature clearance
The record difference does not establish that the shelf is unaffected.
Name the owner
Maya owns the recall assessment.
Compare the full scope
We need the complete product details and notice scope.
Separate records and stock
A delivery entry is not a physical-stock check.
Keep exposure unconfirmed
Patient exposure has not been established.
Protect traceability
Preserve the original references and the results of each check.
Avoid an invented disposition
No disposal or release decision has been supplied.
Follow the real procedure
Use the actual notice and the approved pharmacy process.
Report a mismatch
These identifiers differ; the reason is not yet known.
Keep actions distinct
Holding stock is not the same as destroying it.
Route clinical questions
A pharmacist must address questions about taking the medicine.
Close the record accurately
Record the findings and authorized decision when they are established.
6 language and precision notes
Shows versus confirms
A record shows an entry; confirmation requires the appropriate verification.
Not yet checked
Names the missing work without implying the result.
Four, one
Reads the actual sequence rather than a vague resemblance.
Has not been established
Prevents uncertainty from becoming either reassurance or an alarm.
Complete product details
Expands the comparison beyond one code fragment.
When established
Keeps later decisions out of the current factual record.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which read-back preserves the notice code?

Answer and explanation

D. A, B, four, one

AB41 belongs to the notice; AB14 belongs to the delivery record.

2. Which statement overstates the evidence?

Answer and explanation

A. The shelf is clear because the delivery record shows a different lot.

A delivery entry alone does not prove which lot is physically present or whether stock falls within scope.

3. What should be preserved?

Answer and explanation

C. The source identifiers, actual findings, and authorized decisions

Traceability depends on accurate source references and a record of what was actually checked and decided.

4. Which patient-facing claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

C. Every recall means you should stop taking medicine immediately.

Treatment actions depend on the specific recall and professional advice, not a universal instruction invented from the word recall.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

AB41 is not AB14

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Emil: Pharmacy technician / Maya: Lead pharmacist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Patient exposure
  • Quarantine
  • recall scope
  • lot number
  • release decision
  • physical stock
  • recall notice
  • delivery record
  • traceability
  • disposition
  1. Emil
    Maya, I have a code discrepancy on the recall paperwork. The notice and delivery record look similar, but the final two characters are reversed.
  2. Maya
    Start with the 1. Read its lot exactly, then name the second source. We should not let a familiar-looking code substitute for an exact comparison.
  3. Emil
    The notice says A, B, four, one: AB41. The delivery record says A, B, one, four: AB14. I have not checked the shelf label.
  4. Maya
    The 2 on the notice is AB41; the record shows AB14. Thank you for separating them. The physical label is still an outstanding check.
  5. Emil
    I was about to mark unaffected because AB14 is different. Then I realized I had only checked the delivery entry, not the cartons themselves.
  6. Maya
    It would jump ahead. The 3 is one source. It does not establish which lot is on the shelf or complete the assessment against the notice.
  7. Emil
    Then I will not tell the counter team that the shelf is clear. What should I include when I describe the unresolved query?
  8. Maya
    Name both sources and the unchecked 4. Also identify me as the assessment owner so colleagues know where findings and questions must go.
  9. Emil
    The product has other identifying details as well. I assume the lot code alone is not a reason to skip the product and manufacturer comparison.
  10. Maya
    Correct. We need the complete 5 and applicable product details. Follow the actual notice and our approved process rather than infer the scope from two letters and two digits.
  11. Emil
    If we find a match, should I immediately describe it as proof that a patient received the affected product, or is that another question?
  12. Maya
    That is another question. 6 has not been established. Stock findings and patient-related assessment must be recorded accurately without turning one into an unsupported conclusion about the other.
  13. Emil
    I will attach both source references. Otherwise the next shift may see a corrected summary without understanding why we raised the query.
  14. Maya
    Exactly. Preserve 7 through the source references and actual findings. Anyone reviewing the matter needs to see what was checked, what differed, and what remains unresolved.
  15. Emil
    I also want to avoid using hold, return, and destroy as if they were the same action. The required action depends on the actual instructions.
  16. Maya
    Yes. 8 and final disposition are distinct. Use the applicable process and notice; do not invent disposal or release instructions from this discrepancy.
  17. Emil
    If a patient asks whether to stop taking a medicine while we check this, I should connect that question with the appropriate pharmacist advice.
  18. Maya
    Correct. Do not turn an inventory query into a treatment instruction. An authorized 9 for stock and clinical advice for a patient are different matters.
  19. Emil
    My summary will say AB41 on the notice, AB14 in the delivery record, stock label unchecked, and assessment with Maya. No affected-stock conclusion yet.
  20. Maya
    That is accurate. The 10 is not established either. Bring the verified findings through our process, and keep the pending status clear until the responsible decision is recorded.

Answers and explanations
  1. recall notice Turn 2. The recall notice is the source naming AB41; its identifier must remain distinct from the different delivery entry.
  2. lot number Turn 4. A lot number identifies the production group and must be read in the correct character order to support matching.
  3. delivery record Turn 6. The delivery record documents an entry but does not alone establish the identifier on the stock physically present.
  4. physical stock Turn 8. Physical stock is the inventory actually present; checking it is distinct from reading a delivery entry.
  5. recall scope Turn 10. Recall scope defines the relevant products and limits; a single similar code does not replace the complete notice comparison.
  6. Patient exposure Turn 12. Patient exposure concerns whether someone received or used relevant product and is not established merely by an inventory query.
  7. traceability Turn 14. Traceability depends on retaining source references and verified findings so product history and decisions can be followed.
  8. Quarantine Turn 16. Quarantine controls availability pending an authorized decision; it is not automatically a return, destruction, or final release decision.
  9. disposition Turn 18. Disposition is the decision about the product's handling; it does not authorize a technician to invent patient treatment advice.
  10. release decision Turn 20. A release decision requires the authorized assessment and cannot be inferred from the differing delivery code alone.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Emil: Maya, I have a code discrepancy on the recall paperwork. The notice and delivery record look similar, but the final two characters are reversed.
  2. Maya: Start with the recall notice. Read its lot exactly, then name the second source. We should not let a familiar-looking code substitute for an exact comparison.
  3. Emil: The notice says A, B, four, one: AB41. The delivery record says A, B, one, four: AB14. I have not checked the shelf label.
  4. Maya: The lot number on the notice is AB41; the record shows AB14. Thank you for separating them. The physical label is still an outstanding check.

  12. Maya: That is another question. Patient exposure has not been established. Stock findings and patient-related assessment must be recorded accurately without turning one into an unsupported conclusion about the other.

  14. Maya: Exactly. Preserve traceability through the source references and actual findings. Anyone reviewing the matter needs to see what was checked, what differed, and what remains unresolved.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

AB41 or AB14

Fictional stock-room communication using the recall-identifier case; no handling procedure is enacted.

  1. Technician: The recall notice names AB41, but our delivery record shows AB14. Could you check the difference?
  2. Pharmacist: Yes. Have you compared the complete identifier on the stock label with the notice yet?
  3. Technician: Not yet. I noticed the record difference and brought both references to you first.
  4. Pharmacist: Then affected status isn't established. I'll take the assessment and check the relevant stock record.
  5. Technician: Should I tell the counter team that our entire delivery has been recalled?
  6. Pharmacist: No, that would go beyond the information. Tell them I'm assessing this specific identifier discrepancy.
  7. Technician: The two numbers are easy to reverse when spoken. Shall I repeat them separately?
  8. Pharmacist: Please do, and name the source before each one so I can distinguish them.
  9. Technician: Notice lot number: AB41. Delivery record: AB14. No stock-label match or release decision is confirmed.
  10. Pharmacist: That is clear. I own the assessment; keep the status as under review until I report back.
A damaged carton

Fictional visible outer-carton damage is reported; contents and product suitability have not been assessed.

  1. Technician: The outer carton on delivery D6 is dented. I haven't assessed the contents.
  2. Stock lead: Thanks. Is the dent on every carton in the delivery or only one?
  3. Technician: Only the carton beside the delivery note. The others haven't been included in my observation.
  4. Stock lead: I'll review that item under our quality procedure. Please don't describe the whole delivery as damaged.
  5. Technician: Should I call the product unusable in the stock enquiry, or is that premature?
  6. Stock lead: That's not established. Say visible outer-carton damage, product assessment pending, and identify D6.
  7. Technician: The supplier asks for a description. May I quote those observed facts to them?
  8. Stock lead: Yes, use the agreed supply contact and keep the description limited to what you saw.
  9. Technician: I'll report one dented outer carton, not a damaged medicine or a confirmed cause.
  10. Stock lead: Good. I will communicate the stock decision after assessment so the team knows the actual status.
A count with a defined scope

Fictional inventory query: shelf count is twelve, system shows fourteen; no cause established.

  1. Technician: My count shows twelve units on this shelf, while the inventory screen shows fourteen.
  2. Stock lead: Did you include quarantined stock in the separate area, or only this shelf?
  3. Technician: Only this shelf. I haven't checked whether the screen includes stock in another location.
  4. Stock lead: Then we have a count difference to investigate, not evidence that two units are missing.
  5. Technician: Could you confirm the scope of the system total before I describe the discrepancy?
  6. Stock lead: Yes. I'll check the locations included and compare them with your count record.
  7. Technician: Should I change the total to twelve while you are looking into it?
  8. Stock lead: No adjustment has been approved. Keep the observation and the system total separate for review.
  9. Technician: I'll describe twelve counted on this shelf against fourteen shown, with location scope unresolved.
  10. Stock lead: That gives us a useful starting point without assuming loss, theft, or a counting error.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

AB41 is not AB14
  1. Read AB41 and AB14 character by character, attaching each to the correct source.
  2. Repeat the transfer with CD27 and CD72, the unchecked stock, and Noor.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Read back a different lot discrepancy

Notice lot CD27 differs from delivery entry CD72. Actual stock is unchecked. Pharmacist Noor owns the assessment. No affected-stock conclusion has been reached.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: "The notice names ___."

Answer and explanation

B. CD27

CD27 is the notice identifier, not the different delivery entry.

2. Pharmacist: "The delivery entry is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. CD72

CD72 is the delivery identifier and must not be transposed.

3. Technician: "The actual stock is still ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unchecked

No check of actual stock has yet been established.

4. Pharmacist: "The assessment owner is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Noor

Noor is the explicitly named owner of this assessment.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Course: Pharmacy Technician English
Professional audience: Pharmacy technicians and pharmacy support staff
Scope: All cases, calculations, and product limits are fictional language exercises, not patient-specific directions or clinical certification. Lesson 07: Precise inventory and quality queries
Original fictional case: A fictional recall notice names lot AB41. The delivery record shows AB14, but nobody has checked the label on the stock itself. Technician Emil brings the discrepancy to pharmacist Maya, who owns the recall assessment. The team must compare the actual product and lot with the complete notice through its approved process. Neither affected-stock status nor patient exposure has been established. A different number in one record does not prove the shelf is clear. No disposal, release, patient-contact, or treatment decision is supplied.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Maya: That is accurate. The release decision is not established either. Bring the verified findings through our process, and keep the pending status clear until the responsible decision is recorded.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.
