Dialogue 03: Which prescription needs renewing?
Original fictional setting: Fictional clinic callback about two different prescription references; no medicine instructions are discussed.
Professional roles: Technician / Clinic coordinator
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Technician: I'm following up on renewal request R21. Your message mentions R12, which is a different prescription reference.
Clinic coordinator: Thanks for catching that. I have both requests open, and I quoted the older reference by mistake.
Technician: For R21, our record still shows no response. Does your message contain an answer for that request?
Clinic coordinator: No, it only acknowledges receipt of R21. The prescriber hasn't sent a decision to me yet.
Technician: Then I'll report receipt acknowledged, decision pending. I won't attach the older response to this request.
Clinic coordinator: Please keep the references separate. R12's message belongs to last month's request, not the current renewal.
Technician: The patient expects a status call at noon. Is there a confirmed response time I can relay?
Clinic coordinator: There isn't. I can check our message queue before noon, but that isn't a prescriber completion commitment.
Technician: Understood. Could you make any later message explicit about the reference, so our team can match it?
Clinic coordinator: Yes. I'll identify R21 in the subject and use our usual secure communication route for the details.
Technician: I'll leave today's renewal pending and tell the patient we have acknowledgment, not a new prescription.
Clinic coordinator: That's accurate. Thank you for checking the reference before treating my earlier message as an answer.
Vocabulary in this exchange: prescription: An order for a medicine issued by a prescriber, subject to the pharmacy's required checks. / prescriber: A healthcare professional permitted to issue the medicine order concerned. / renewal request: A request to a prescriber for a new or renewed prescription authorization.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Pharmacy Technician English
Professional audience: Pharmacy technicians and pharmacy support staff
Scope: All cases, calculations, and product limits are fictional language exercises, not patient-specific directions or clinical certification. Follow local law, authorized duties, pharmacist direction, privacy procedures, and actual recall notices. Urgent concerns need the appropriate prompt response, not a routine update window. US and UK sources provide context, not interchangeable rules. This book does not teach prescribing, dose selection, or clinical counseling.
Dialogue 03: Which prescription needs renewing?
Original fictional setting: Fictional clinic callback about two different prescription references; no medicine instructions are discussed.
Professional roles: Technician / Clinic coordinator
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Technician: I'm following up on renewal request R21. Your message mentions R12, which is a different prescription reference.
Clinic coordinator: Thanks for catching that. I have both requests open, and I quoted the older reference by mistake.
Technician: For R21, our record still shows no response. Does your message contain an answer for that request?
Clinic coordinator: No, it only acknowledges receipt of R21. The prescriber hasn't sent a decision to me yet.
Technician: Then I'll report receipt acknowledged, decision pending. I won't attach the older response to this request.
Clinic coordinator: Please keep the references separate. R12's message belongs to last month's request, not the current renewal.
Technician: The patient expects a status call at noon. Is there a confirmed response time I can relay?
Clinic coordinator: There isn't. I can check our message queue before noon, but that isn't a prescriber completion commitment.
Technician: Understood. Could you make any later message explicit about the reference, so our team can match it?
Clinic coordinator: Yes. I'll identify R21 in the subject and use our usual secure communication route for the details.
Technician: I'll leave today's renewal pending and tell the patient we have acknowledgment, not a new prescription.
Clinic coordinator: That's accurate. Thank you for checking the reference before treating my earlier message as an answer.
Vocabulary in this exchange: prescription: An order for a medicine issued by a prescriber, subject to the pharmacy's required checks. / prescriber: A healthcare professional permitted to issue the medicine order concerned. / renewal request: A request to a prescriber for a new or renewed prescription authorization.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 04: A supplier revises its estimate

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 04: A supplier revises its estimate
Original fictional setting: Fictional wholesaler call: Thursday replaces Tuesday as the predicted delivery date; the cause is unknown.
Professional roles: Technician / Wholesaler representative
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Technician: Yesterday's update estimated Tuesday; today's says Thursday. Which date should I quote to our stock lead?
Wholesaler representative: Thursday is the current estimate. Tuesday has been superseded, and I can't confirm shipment at this stage.
Technician: Can you tell me whether the change concerns our entire order or only the line marked P7?
Wholesaler representative: Only P7 has changed. The other lines keep their existing statuses; please don't apply Thursday to all items.
Technician: Thanks. I also need to correct a patient-facing message that referred to Tuesday as if it were certain.
Wholesaler representative: Please describe Thursday as an estimate too. We don't have a carrier confirmation that would support stronger wording.
Technician: Is a supplier shortage confirmed as the cause, or would that be an inference?
Wholesaler representative: It would be an inference. The notice changes the date but doesn't give a verified cause.
Technician: I'll report the changed estimate and leave the cause unspecified. When is your next update?
Wholesaler representative: Our next scheduled stock update is tomorrow morning. It may still show the same estimate rather than dispatch.
Technician: I'll check then and ask the pharmacy team to correct the earlier collection expectation separately.
Wholesaler representative: That keeps the messages accurate: P7's estimate changed, its shipment remains unconfirmed, and other lines are unchanged.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 05: A consultation needs language support
Original fictional setting: Fictional pharmacy arranges an interpreter for a label discussion; no medicine advice is supplied.
Professional roles: Patient / Technician
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Patient: I can manage payment in English, but I need language support for the pharmacist's explanation.
Technician: Thank you for telling me. Which language would you prefer for that discussion about your prescription label?
Patient: Arabic, please. My daughter is here, but I would prefer not to make her explain medical words.
Technician: I'll ask Ravi about the pharmacy's interpreter service. We can keep the payment details separate meanwhile.
Patient: Please tell him I understand the medicine name; the problem is the longer phrase on this label.
Technician: I'll identify that phrase as your question, without paraphrasing the advice or asking your daughter to translate it.
Patient: Will the interpreter join the pharmacist and me, or explain a message afterward?
Technician: Ravi will confirm the arrangement. Our service contact is checking availability now; no start time is confirmed.
Patient: I can wait for a status update, but I need to leave before the school pickup.
Technician: I'll include that timing constraint. Ravi has accepted the consultation and will discuss the available support with you.
Patient: That's useful. I don't want a quick answer I might misunderstand just because people are waiting.
Technician: The consultation counter is available for your privacy. Let's take the label question there when Ravi joins you.
Vocabulary in this exchange: prescription: An order for a medicine issued by a prescriber, subject to the pharmacy's required checks.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 06: Permission for a conversation is not every permission
Original fictional setting: Fictional patient invites a sibling into a pharmacist conversation but asks about separate future collection arrangements.
Professional roles: Patient / Technician
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Patient: I'd like my brother present for the pharmacist's explanation, but I have another privacy question.
Technician: Please tell me the question. We can ask Maya to clarify the arrangements before the conversation begins.
Patient: Does inviting him into today's discussion automatically let him collect every prescription for me in future?
Technician: We shouldn't treat those as the same arrangement. Today's discussion and future collection require their own clarification.
Patient: Good. I want his help today, but haven't decided about future collections or telephone updates.
Technician: I'll tell Maya exactly that distinction. I won't describe your request as unrestricted permission for other matters.
Patient: Can you also avoid changing my contact preference to his number just because he's with me?
Technician: Yes. Your request concerns today's conversation; I have not changed the telephone number on your profile.
Patient: He may ask questions during the discussion. I want to be included rather than have everyone talk around me.
Technician: I'll pass that on too: you want his support, with the pharmacist continuing to address you directly.
Patient: That captures it. I'd like to discuss any future collection arrangement separately when we have more time.
Technician: Maya is ready to join us. I'll introduce today's request and leave those future arrangements explicitly undecided.
Vocabulary in this exchange: prescription: An order for a medicine issued by a prescriber, subject to the pharmacy's required checks. / contact preference: A patient's recorded choice about suitable ways to communicate with them.
END REFERENCE

Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: Pharmacy Technician English
Professional audience: Pharmacy technicians and pharmacy support staff
Scope: All cases, calculations, and product limits are fictional language exercises, not patient-specific directions or clinical certification. US and UK sources provide context, not interchangeable rules. This book does not teach prescribing, dose selection, or clinical counseling.
Dialogue 06: Permission for a conversation is not every permission
Original fictional setting: Fictional patient invites a sibling into a pharmacist conversation but asks about separate future collection arrangements.
Professional roles: Patient / Technician
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Patient: I'd like my brother present for the pharmacist's explanation, but I have another privacy question.
Technician: Please tell me the question. We can ask Maya to clarify the arrangements before the conversation begins.
Patient: Does inviting him into today's discussion automatically let him collect every prescription for me in future?
Technician: We shouldn't treat those as the same arrangement. Today's discussion and future collection require their own clarification.
Patient: Good. I want his help today, but haven't decided about future collections or telephone updates.
Technician: I'll tell Maya exactly that distinction. I won't describe your request as unrestricted permission for other matters.
Patient: Can you also avoid changing my contact preference to his number just because he's with me?
Technician: Yes. Your request concerns today's conversation; I have not changed the telephone number on your profile.
Patient: He may ask questions during the discussion. I want to be included rather than have everyone talk around me.
Technician: I'll pass that on too: you want his support, with the pharmacist continuing to address you directly.
Patient: That captures it. I'd like to discuss any future collection arrangement separately when we have more time.
Technician: Maya is ready to join us. I'll introduce today's request and leave those future arrangements explicitly undecided.
Vocabulary in this exchange: prescription: An order for a medicine issued by a prescriber, subject to the pharmacy's required checks. / contact preference: A patient's recorded choice about suitable ways to communicate with them.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 07: An expiry-date query in a delivery record
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 07: An expiry-date query in a delivery record
Original fictional setting: Fictional stock documentation discussion; a scanned date is unclear and no product-use decision is made.
Professional roles: Technician / Stock lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Technician: The delivery scan makes the expiry date difficult to read. One digit could be three or eight.
Stock lead: Which source are you looking at: the supplier's digital record or a photograph of the package?
Technician: A photograph attached to delivery E9. The image is blurred where the last digit appears.
Stock lead: Then we need a legible source, not an interpretation of the blur. I'll take the verification.
Technician: Could you also confirm whether the date belongs to E9 rather than the adjacent line?
Stock lead: Yes. The line association matters as much as the digit. Neither detail is confirmed yet.
Technician: The receiving note calls it expired. I think that wording goes beyond what the image shows.
Stock lead: Agreed. Report the unclear date and my pending check; don't substitute a different definite quality claim.
Technician: I'll tell the colleague who used expired that the source is unreadable, not proof of expiry.
Stock lead: Please do. We need everyone using the same status while I review the package information.
Technician: I'll leave the use decision with you and identify the photograph as the source of the uncertainty.
Stock lead: That is enough for this handoff: delivery E9, unclear date, line association unconfirmed, and my review pending.
Vocabulary in this exchange: expiry date: The labeled date beyond which a product is not to be used under its stated conditions.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Extend the conversation library

Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Dialogue 08: A correction must reach the listener

Dialogue 08: A correction must reach the listener
Original fictional setting: Fictional pharmacy debrief: an internal note was corrected, but a patient still has an inaccurate delivery date.
Professional roles: Pharmacist / Technician
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Pharmacist: The record now says delivery unconfirmed. Has the patient heard that correction, or only the afternoon team?
Technician: Only the team. The patient still has my earlier message giving Friday as a definite date.
Pharmacist: Then the record is more accurate, but the communication problem remains. Who can contact the patient today?
Technician: I can, using the recorded preference. I'll identify my earlier wording rather than blame the supplier.
Pharmacist: What will you say if the patient asks whether the corrected date is now Saturday?
Technician: There isn't a confirmed replacement date. I'll say the Friday guarantee was wrong and arrival remains unconfirmed.
Pharmacist: Good. Please acknowledge that the patient may have arranged transport, without assuming whether they actually did.
Technician: I'll ask whether the message affected their plans and explain the next supplier-status check at noon tomorrow.
Pharmacist: Can you own tomorrow's update as well, or do we need a handoff before you leave?
Technician: I'm scheduled here tomorrow and can own it. I'll keep that contact separate from the stock enquiry.
Pharmacist: Then the outstanding tasks are today's correction call and tomorrow's update, not a promised delivery.
Technician: I'll make today's call now and record what was communicated, including any contact attempt that doesn't connect.
END REFERENCE

REFERENCE
Course: Pharmacy Technician English
Professional audience: Pharmacy technicians and pharmacy support staff
Scope: All cases, calculations, and product limits are fictional language exercises, not patient-specific directions or clinical certification. Follow local law, authorized duties, pharmacist direction, privacy procedures, and actual recall notices. Urgent concerns need the appropriate prompt response, not a routine update window. US and UK sources provide context, not interchangeable rules. This book does not teach prescribing, dose selection, or clinical counseling.
Lesson 01: Prescription intake without assumptions
Original fictional case: Patient Ellis asks technician Nia to complete a prescription order whose strength field is unclear. Ellis remembers a blue label and says the prescriber usually orders the same thing. Nia cannot select the strength. Pharmacist Maya can take the clarification, but no completion time is known. Nia must distinguish remembered information from verified current instructions, explain the unresolved field, and route the question. No medicine name, dose, or numerical strength is provided in this teaching case, and the exercise does not ask the learner to infer one.
Language workshop: A familiar label does not resolve an unclear strength
Communication goal: Identify an ambiguous order field and arrange pharmacist clarification without selecting a strength from memory or appearance.
Language guidance: Strength and dose are not synonyms. A product may contain a particular amount per unit, while the prescribed administration uses a specified amount and schedule. This unit supplies neither and does not authorize calculation or selection from a remembered label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The strength field on this order is unclear. / I understand that you usually receive the same medicine. / The label color is useful context, but it does not confirm the current strength. / I cannot select a strength from an unclear entry. / Pharmacist Maya can take the clarification. / We need the order clarified; I am not asking you to guess it. / I will record that you remember a blue label. / Strength describes the product; dose concerns the prescribed amount used. / We need the current instructions confirmed, not just an earlier pattern. / The pharmacist can review the relevant previous record. / I do not yet have a confirmed completion time. / The clarification is pending, not completed. / Can Maya confirm receipt of this question? / Any question about how to take it should go to the pharmacist. / Please tell the pharmacist about any concern that needs prompt attention. / We will use the verified instruction rather than fill the gap with a guess.
Vocabulary: prescription: An authorized order for a medicine under the applicable rules. / intake: Receiving and organizing information needed to process an order. / prescriber: A professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine. / pharmacist: A qualified medicines professional with the relevant practice authority. / strength: The amount of active ingredient in a stated unit or quantity of the product. / dose: The amount prescribed for a particular administration. / dosage form: The physical form of the product, such as a tablet or liquid. / active ingredient: The substance intended to produce the medicine's therapeutic effect. / concentration: The amount of a substance within a specified quantity of a preparation. / route: The specified way a medicine is taken or applied. / frequency: How often a prescribed action or administration occurs. / directions for use: The authorized instructions for taking or using the medicine. / quantity: The amount of product specified for supply. / days supply: Days represented by quantity and verified directions under the relevant calculation rules; not proof of actual use. / brand name: A trade name for a particular product. / generic name: The recognized nonproprietary name of the medicine substance. / product identifier: A code or reference identifying a specified product. / NDC: National Drug Code; a United States drug product identification system. / order field: A defined part of the order record. / ambiguous entry: Information that permits more than one interpretation. / clarification request: A request to resolve an unclear or missing instruction. / current order: The order presently being processed, distinct from past records. / historical record: Information about a previous order or event. / verified instruction: A direction confirmed through the appropriate process.
Speaking task: Read the strength question aloud without guessing a number or naming a medicine. Complete the transfer with dosage form, the reported box description, the accepting pharmacist, and the actual timing status. Check the four answers, then repeat the handoff while keeping memory separate from a verified current instruction.
Partner: Ellis: Patient / Nia: Pharmacy technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A current order has an unclear dosage-form field. The patient remembers a white box. Pharmacist Ren accepts the clarification. No product form or completion time has been confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ellis: If I have a question about how to take the medicine once the order is clarified, should I ask Maya that as well?
Nia: Yes. Questions about the directions for use need the pharmacist's appropriate explanation. Resolving the strength field does not mean every question about your medicine has already been answered.
Ellis: Thank you. Please tell Maya that I remember a blue label and a usual pattern, but that I have not supplied a confirmed current strength.
Nia: I will preserve that in intake. The strength remains unclear, Maya is the clarification route, and no completion time is confirmed. We will not fill the gap with an assumption.
END REFERENCE

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.

Language notes and further reading
